On the Effect of (Near) Duplicate Subwords in Language Modelling
Anton Schäfer, Thomas Hofmann, Imanol Schlag, Tiago Pimentel

Abstract
Tokenisation is a core part of language models (LMs). It involves splitting a character sequence into subwords which are assigned arbitrary indices before being served to the LM. While typically lossless, however, this process may lead to less sample efficient LM training: as it removes character-level information, it could make it harder for LMs to generalise across similar subwords, such as now and Now . We refer to such subwords as near duplicates . In this paper, we study the impact of near duplicate subwords on LM training efficiency. First, we design an experiment that gives us an upper bound to how much we should expect a model to improve if we could perfectly generalise across near duplicates. We do this by duplicating each subword in our LM’s vocabulary, creating perfectly equivalent classes of subwords. Experimentally, we find that LMs need roughly 17% more data when trained in a fully duplicated setting. Second, we investigate the impact of naturally occurring near duplicates on LMs. Here, we see that merging them considerably hurts LM performance. Therefore, although subword duplication negatively impacts LM training efficiency, naturally occurring near duplicates may not be as similar as anticipated, limiting the potential for performance improvements. antonschafer/duplicate-subwords



1
Introduction

Most modern language models (LMs) do not have direct access to the bits or characters which make up the text that they must model.
Rather, they operate on higher-level units, so-called tokens, which are elements of a finite set of previously defined
subwords
.
This set of subwords is typically obtained as the output of a tokenisation method and forms an LM’s
vocabulary

Gage 1994
;
Sennrich et al. 2016
;
Kudo 2018
;
Wu et al. 2016
.
Importantly, most tokenisation algorithms are lossless: the original character sequence is perfectly recoverable from its tokenised version.

Model

Near Duplicate Rate

GPT-{3.5, 4, 4-turbo}

43%

Claude 2.1

46%

Llama 1 & 2

35%

Mistral 7B & 8x7B

37%

Gemma 7B

39%

Table 1:
Near duplicate rates of modern LLM vocabularies. We consider subwords that only differ in whitespace, capitalization, or plural suffix as equivalent (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{all}}
deduplication mapping, see
section

5.2.1
). For details, see
appendix

B
.

A language model’s vocabulary, however, may contain several
near duplicate subwords
:
minimal pairs like
now
and
Now
, with roughly the same semantic meaning, but which differ due to typos, whitespace marking, or capitalisation
Stanić et al. 2023
.
Such near duplicates can make up over 40% of the vocabulary of modern LMs (see
table

1
).
Intuitively, if the model had access to character-level information, it should trivially generalise what it learns from one of these forms to the other.
Given only access to subword-level inputs, however, the model may not be able to do the same, or may require more data to do so.

Previous work tried to address this issue by
modelling language directly at the character, byte, or even pixel level
(
Kim et al. 2016
;
Clark et al. 2022
;
Xue et al. 2022
;
Yu et al. 2023
;
Rust et al. 2023
,
inter alia
)
.
1
1

1

These works are not solely motivated by near duplicates.
Other commonly named advantages of character/byte-level models include: the possibility of optimising them end-to-end without relying on a two-stage approach, greater flexibility by not committing to a (potentially suboptimal) tokeniser,
and more direct access to word forms which might be relevant in, e.g., word-play related tasks
(
Rozner et al. 2021
)
.

However, while the existing literature has proposed several solutions to improve LMs generalisation across near duplicates, a proper quantification of the issue is still lacking.

In this work, we thus take a step back and assess the actual impact of near duplicate subwords on LMs’ performance.
To this end, we first propose a controlled synthetic setting where we duplicate every subword in our LM’s vocabulary, allowing us to
(1.)
quantify an LM’s ability to generalise across perfectly equivalent duplicates and to
(2.)
carefully investigate how the generalisation happens.
This yields an upper bound on the cost incurred due to limited generalisation across real near duplicates: a vocabulary with 40% duplicates (common for LLMs, see
table

1
) may reduce data efficiency by up to 10%.
We then
(3.)
investigate the tightness of this upper bound by merging naturally occurring near duplicates in an LM’s vocabulary.
We find that deduplicating the vocabulary in this way, in general, hurts performance instead of improving it.
This suggests that real near duplicates might be less similar than anticipated, which might impose challenges when trying to leverage their similarity to improve LMs’ performance.

2
Language Modelling

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\Sigma}
be a vocabulary of subwords.
A language model
\widehat{p}
is formally defined as a probability distribution over the set of all finite sequences of subwords
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}=({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{1},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{2},...)\in{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathcal{W}}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\Sigma}^{*}
:
2
2

2

We define
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\Sigma}
with a special end-of-sequence symbol (
eos
). Any string with mid-sequence
eos
is assigned probability zero.

\displaystyle\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})=\prod_{t=1}^{|{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}|}\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t})

(1)

where
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t})
is the probability of token
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{t}
given the sequence of previous tokens
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{0},...{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{t-1}
.

In order for
\widehat{p}
to approximate the true distributions over natural strings
p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
, we train this model to minimise its cross-entropy with
p
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{H}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}})=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathcal{W}}}p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})\sum_{t=1}^{|{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}|}\log\frac{1}{\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t})}

(2)

where
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}
represents a
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathcal{W}}
-valued random variable.
Since we do not know
p
, we approximate this objective using a finite training set
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{trn}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{N}\sim p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
.
This leads to the loss function:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}),\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{trn}})=\frac{1}{N}\!\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{trn}}}\!\sum_{t=1}^{|{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}|}\log\frac{1}{\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t})}

(3)

3
Subword Duplication

Now, let’s assume there exist in our alphabet pairs or groups of subwords which are nearly identical—in both their orthography and semantics.
Such near duplicates can arise from various sources, including but not limited to
capitalization differences (e.g.,
now
vs.
Now
),
typographical errors (
language
vs.
langauge
),
the presence or absence of whitespace (e.g.,
the
vs.
_the
),
3
3

3

We denote spaces as
‘_’
for readability.

and variations in spelling (e.g.,
modeling
vs.
modelling
).
The question we are concerned with is: how might such subword duplication affect the performance of language models?

To define this question formally, let
\mathcal{S}
be a set of disjoint sets of near duplicate subwords:

\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{458752}{458752}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\hbox to5.64pt{\vbox to5.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.81865pt\lower-2.81865pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.62 0 C 3.62 2 2 3.62 0 3.62 C -2 3.62 -3.62 2 -3.62 0 C -3.62 -2 -2 -3.62 0 -3.62 C 2 -3.62 3.62 -2 3.62 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.4861pt}{-1.16875pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}}}}\mid 0<i\leq|\mathcal{S}|\}
be a set of
canonical symbols
which we will use to represent each of these duplicate sets.
To find out how duplication affects LMs, we can create a map
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}:{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\Sigma}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\overline{\Sigma}}
which deduplicates subwords, mapping duplicates to the corresponding canonical symbols.
This map is defined as:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}&\mathrm{if}\,\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}\notin\mathrm{flatten}(\mathcal{S})\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{458752}{458752}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox to8.35pt{\vbox to8.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.1751pt\lower-4.1751pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.5 0 C 5.5 3.04 3.04 5.5 0 5.5 C -3.04 5.5 -5.5 3.04 -5.5 0 C -5.5 -3.04 -3.04 -5.5 0 -5.5 C 3.04 -5.5 5.5 -3.04 5.5 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.13194pt}{-2.35417pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{458752}{458752}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\hbox to8.35pt{\vbox to8.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.1751pt\lower-4.1751pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.5 0 C 5.5 3.04 3.04 5.5 0 5.5 C -3.04 5.5 -5.5 3.04 -5.5 0 C -5.5 -3.04 -3.04 -5.5 0 -5.5 C 3.04 -5.5 5.5 -3.04 5.5 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.13194pt}{-2.35417pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{458752}{458752}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\hbox to6.65pt{\vbox to6.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.32426pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.32 0 C 4.32 2.39 2.39 4.32 0 4.32 C -2.39 4.32 -4.32 2.39 -4.32 0 C -4.32 -2.39 -2.39 -4.32 0 -4.32 C 2.39 -4.32 4.32 -2.39 4.32 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.68056pt}{-1.63625pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{458752}{458752}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\hbox to5.64pt{\vbox to5.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.81865pt\lower-2.81865pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.62 0 C 3.62 2 2 3.62 0 3.62 C -2 3.62 -3.62 2 -3.62 0 C -3.62 -2 -2 -3.62 0 -3.62 C 2 -3.62 3.62 -2 3.62 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.4861pt}{-1.16875pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}}}}&\mathrm{if}\,\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{458752}{458752}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox to8.35pt{\vbox to8.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.1751pt\lower-4.1751pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.5 0 C 5.5 3.04 3.04 5.5 0 5.5 C -3.04 5.5 -5.5 3.04 -5.5 0 C -5.5 -3.04 -3.04 -5.5 0 -5.5 C 3.04 -5.5 5.5 -3.04 5.5 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.13194pt}{-2.35417pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{458752}{458752}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\hbox to8.35pt{\vbox to8.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.1751pt\lower-4.1751pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.5 0 C 5.5 3.04 3.04 5.5 0 5.5 C -3.04 5.5 -5.5 3.04 -5.5 0 C -5.5 -3.04 -3.04 -5.5 0 -5.5 C 3.04 -5.5 5.5 -3.04 5.5 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.13194pt}{-2.35417pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{458752}{458752}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\hbox to6.65pt{\vbox to6.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.32426pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.32 0 C 4.32 2.39 2.39 4.32 0 4.32 C -2.39 4.32 -4.32 2.39 -4.32 0 C -4.32 -2.39 -2.39 -4.32 0 -4.32 C 2.39 -4.32 4.32 -2.39 4.32 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.68056pt}{-1.63625pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{458752}{458752}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\hbox to5.64pt{\vbox to5.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.81865pt\lower-2.81865pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.62 0 C 3.62 2 2 3.62 0 3.62 C -2 3.62 -3.62 2 -3.62 0 C -3.62 -2 -2 -3.62 0 -3.62 C 2 -3.62 3.62 -2 3.62 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.4861pt}{-1.16875pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}}}}\end{array}\right.

with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\overline{\Sigma}}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\Sigma}\setminus\mathrm{flatten}(\mathcal{S}))\cup\overline{\mathcal{S}}
.
We are now in a position to define a distribution over deduplicated subword sequences:

\displaystyle p({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathcal{W}}}p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})\,\mathbbm{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})\}

(8)

where we overload
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}
to operate on sequences
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}
by applying it elementwise on each subword
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{t}\in{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}
.
Note that
p({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
is now a distribution over deduplicated subword sequences
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}=({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{1},{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{2},...)\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{C}}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\overline{\Sigma}}^{*}
.
We can further define a hybrid conditional
projected distribution
as:

\displaystyle p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t})=

(9)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}\in{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\Sigma}}p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t})\,\mathbbm{1}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{t}={\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w})\}

In words, the conditional probability
p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t})
of a deduplicated subword
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{t}
is defined as the sum of the conditional probabilities of all subwords which map to it through
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w})
.

3.1
Comparing (De-)Duplicated LMs

Typically, LMs are evaluated based on their cross-entropy (or perplexity) on a held-out test set.
It would, however, be unfair to simply compare the cross-entropies of LMs trained on
p({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
and
p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
.
The cross-entropy is lower bounded by the entropy—with a perfect model’s cross-entropy equalling the entropy.
If
p({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
’s and
p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
’s entropies are different, they would impose different optima achievable by LMs trained in each setting.
We write these distributions’ entropies as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}}p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})\sum_{t=1}^{|{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}|}\log\frac{1}{p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t})}

(10)

and
\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}})
, analogously,
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}
denotes a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{C}}
-valued random variable.
Now note that, given their definition in Eq.
8
, deduplicated sequences
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}
are deterministic given the original subwords
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}
.
The two entropies above are thus related via equation:

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}})=\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}})+\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}})

(11)

Assuming
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}
cannot be deterministically predicted from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}
, we have that
\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}})>0
;
this implies that predicting
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}
is strictly harder than
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}
.

To make these settings more easily comparable, we define
p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
’s
projected entropy
as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathcal{W}}}p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})\sum_{t=1}^{|{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}|}\log\frac{1}{p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{t})\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t})}

(12)

This entropy measures the uncertainty in predicting a deduplicated subword
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{t}
given the duplicated context
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t}
.
Interestingly, we can show that:

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}})-\mathrm{MI}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}};{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{\mathrm{T}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}})

(13)

where
\mathrm{MI}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}};{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{\mathrm{T}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}})
is the mutual information between a subword context
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t}
and the next deduplicated token
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{t}
conditioned on the previous deduplicated tokens
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}_{<t}
(see
appendix

A
for a proof and the precise definition of this mutual information).
In both
section

5.1
and
section

5.2
, we discuss how this value relates to our research question.

4
Experimental Setup

We implement all of our experiments in the codebase of the Languini Kitchen
(
Stanić et al. 2023
)
.
Below, we provide an overview of the models and datasets used here.
We refer the reader to
Stanić et al. 2023
’s (
Stanić et al. 2023
) work for more details regarding implementation choices, training setup, and dataset collection.

Model.

We use the GPT model from Languini, which is a GPT-2 style transformer decoder
Radford et al. 2019
.
Unless otherwise noted, we use the “small” configuration with 12 layers, hidden size 768, and 85M non-embedding parameters.
We train models with sequence length 512, batch size 128, the Adam optimiser
Kingma and Ba 2015
, and a cosine learning rate schedule from 6e-4 to 6e-6 with 500 warmup steps.

Data.

We train on the Languini training data, a filtered version of the books3 subset from the Pile
Gao et al. 2020
.
For evaluation, we use the held-out Languini test set, which contains 11M tokens.
This data is pre-tokenised into a vocabulary of size 16k using a BPE tokeniser
Gage 1994
;
Sennrich et al. 2016
trained using SentencePiece
Kudo and Richardson 2018
.
Unless otherwise noted, we train our models for 18,265 steps—i.e., the first 1.2B tokens in our dataset—which corresponds to training the small GPT model for 6h on an RTX 3090 GPU; this is Languini’s GPT small 6h setting.

Evaluation.

We generally report our model’s perplexity on the test set as our evaluation metric.
To ensure sufficient context for all predictions, we use a sliding window with steps of 128: we fill in a 512 tokens context, ignore the model’s outputs on the initial 384, and evaluate it only using the last 128 tokens.
For models
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
trained on the deduplicated setting, we simply report their perplexities, defined as the exponentiated cross-entropy evaluated on a held-out test set
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{eval}}
:

\mathrm{PPL}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}})=\exp\left(\mathcal{L}(\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}});\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{eval}})\right)
.
When evaluating models
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
, trained in the duplicated setting,
we report their
projected perplexity
, defined as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{PPL}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}})=\exp\left(\mathcal{L}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}(\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}});\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{eval}})\right)

(14)

\displaystyle\quad=\exp\!\!\left(\!\frac{1}{N}\!\!\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{eval}}}\!\sum_{t=1}^{|{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}|}\log\frac{1}{\widehat{p}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{t})\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t})}\right)

where
\widehat{p}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}
is defined analogously to
p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}
(see Eq.
9
).
Intuitively, we add up the probabilities of subwords
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}
that are equivalent under
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}
(i.e., which map to the same canonical symbol
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}
), to avoid giving
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
an unfair advantage over
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
.

5
Experiments and Results

We evaluate our LM’s ability to generalise over (near) duplicates in two settings: perfect and natural duplication.
In the perfect duplication setting,
we compare LMs trained using either the default or a synthetically duplicated vocabulary; this gives us an upper bound for the impact of real near duplicates, as synthetically duplicated subwords are perfectly comparable in terms of their semantics.
In the natural duplication setting, we deduplicate the default vocabulary by merging real near duplicates.
By comparing performance on the default and the deduplicated vocabulary, we verify whether the effect of natural near duplicates in LMs is comparable to the effect of perfect duplicates.

Figure 1:
Left: Fitted power laws capturing the relationship between training data and
\mathrm{PPL}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}
. Our standard training set contains around
1.2
B tokens. Right: Data required to achieve the same performance with
p({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
and
p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
, computed based on the fitted scaling law curves. In the considered interval, this curve’s slope—which roughly corresponds to
\frac{\text{number of training tokens for $p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})$}}{\text{number of training tokens for $p({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})$}}
—is approximately equal to
\frac{1}{0.85}
.

5.1
Perfect Duplication

In this first set of experiments, we assume an idealised situation where all subwords in a near duplicate set
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{458752}{458752}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox to8.35pt{\vbox to8.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.1751pt\lower-4.1751pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{458752}{458752}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\hbox to5.64pt{\vbox to5.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.81865pt\lower-2.81865pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.62 0 C 3.62 2 2 3.62 0 3.62 C -2 3.62 -3.62 2 -3.62 0 C -3.62 -2 -2 -3.62 0 -3.62 C 2 -3.62 3.62 -2 3.62 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.4861pt}{-1.16875pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}}}}
are perfectly interchangeable with each other.
Choosing among these subwords, then, is neither impacted by prior subwords nor impacts future subword choices.
In this case, we can relate distributions
p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
and
p({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
as:

\displaystyle p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{t=1}^{|{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}|}p({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}_{<t})\!\!\underbrace{p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{t})}_{\text{duplicate choice}}

(15)

Further, we can show that in this idealised setting
\mathrm{MI}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}};{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{\mathrm{T}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}})=0
; this is because the decomposition above implies conditional independence between
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{t}
and any
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{t^{\prime}}
given
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{t}
.
We thus have:

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}})

(16)

which creates a perfectly controlled setting to evaluate language models.
If we could train a perfect language model on either distribution
p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
or
p({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
, we would achieve the same performance in both settings.
Any difference in language modelling performance between these settings must thus derive from a language model’s lack of ability to generalise from observing near duplicate subwords.

. Lower is better. The right column shows results for Languini’s GPT-medium model with 370M parameters (vs 111M for small), trained on 2.8B tokens (vs 1.2B for small).

5.1.1
Empirical Implementation

To achieve the perfect duplication described above, we simulate Eq.
15
by duplicating every entry in our subword vocabulary.
First, we assume our BPE-generated vocabulary is composed of canonical symbols
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\overline{\Sigma}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{458752}{458752}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox to9.22pt{\vbox to9.22pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.60942pt\lower-4.60942pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$}}}}}}
is seen by
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
.
However, we achieve significantly better performance with
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
than with
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
trained on only 50% of the dataset (see
table

2
).
The model thus seems to generalise across duplicates, with data containing
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}
leading to improved performance on
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}^{\prime}
and vice versa.
Still, duplication significantly hurts performance.
Using the duplicated data, the model
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
is only about
85\%
as data efficient as
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
, which is trained on the original data.
This suggests our LMs cannot generalise perfectly.
Interestingly, this trend stays relatively consistent across different amounts of training data (up to 3x, see
fig.

1
) and also applies to a 3x larger GPT-medium model (see
table

2
). Further, if we vary the number of subwords we duplicate, interpolating from 0% of the vocabulary (
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
setting) to 100% of the vocabulary (
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
setting), we obtain a roughly linear increase in
\mathrm{PPL}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}
(see
fig.

2
).

Takeaway 1
.

The model is capable of generalising across duplicates, yet their presence negatively impacts performance.

Figure 2:

Impact of duplication on
\mathrm{PPL}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}
while varying the fraction of subwords in the vocabulary that are duplicated (
1.0
corresponds to
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
,
0.0
to
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}
)). Lower
\mathrm{PPL}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}
is better. When duplicating 70% of the vocabulary (which yields a 41% duplication rate in the final vocabulary, roughly the rate of near duplicates in real vocabularies), we obtain
\mathrm{PPL}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}\approx 22.4
; this is equivalent to a
\approx 10\%
decrease in data efficiency.

is to fully align their representations.
If these word pairs have a cosine similarity of
1.0
, then any change to other model components would affect them similarly.
When we analyse our model
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
’s embeddings, we indeed observe a high average cosine similarity of around 0.8 among duplicate pairs.
This number is even higher for frequent subwords (see
fig.

3
);
it appears that a subword’s frequency during training is an underlying driver for alignment.
This observation is intuitive: the representations of

are both randomly initialised and converge to each other after a certain number of gradient updates.

What causes this alignment of representations and what is it impacted by?
Loosely speaking, the contexts in which the duplicates appear follow the same distribution; this might lead to similar gradient signals throughout training.
If this is the case, then this high cosine similarity should not be an exclusive property of transformers, but apply to simpler architectures as well.
Interestingly, when training a word2vec model
Mikolov et al. 2013
on the same data, we observe its embeddings exhibit even stronger alignment (details in
appendix

E
).

Takeaway 2
.

Representations of frequent duplicates have high cosine similarity.

Figure 3:
Input embedding cosine similarity of duplicates

5.1.4
Finetuning Generalisation

Presumably, the alignment of duplicated word pairs may cause the model to use the same “circuits” when processing those words, allowing it to generalise what it learns across them
Cammarata et al. 2020
;
Elhage et al. 2021
.
To verify this hypothesis, we finetune our
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
model on GLUE
Wang et al. 2019
, employing only one subword from each duplicate pair as input (specifically,
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{458752}{458752}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox to8.35pt{\vbox to8.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.1751pt\lower-4.1751pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

5.1.5
Comparing Duplicating or Not a Pair

We observed that the alignment of duplicate subword representations seems to drive generalisation.
If infrequent subwords have less aligned representations, does this mean that they generalise less?
To investigate the effects of duplication in a more controlled manner, we now duplicate only half of our vocabulary and train a model in this setting.
This gives us a treatment group of duplicated subwords and a control group of non-duplicated subwords, allowing us to isolate the causal effect of duplication.
We will now analyse this effect on the output side (predicting duplicated tokens compared to non-duplicated tokens) and on the input side (predictions based on a context with many duplicated tokens vs few duplicated tokens).

Subset

Mean
\mathbf{\Delta}
Surprisal

All

0.015

Duplicated

0.018

Not Duplicated

0.012

Table 4:
Duplication of half the vocabulary: mean difference in surprisal to
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
for subwords within the treatment (duplicated) and control (non-duplicated) groups.

Figure 4:

Duplication of half of the vocabulary: Analysing the mean surprisal difference per subword between
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
and
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
.
Frequencies are categorised into bins, with averages computed for each bin.
6
6

6

To reduce noise and ensure readability, we only consider subwords that occur at least 10 times in the test set and bins that contain at least 3 subwords.

Figure 5:

Duplication of half of the vocabulary. Difference between the surprisal assigned to each token by
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
and
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
, depending on fraction
\frac{\text{duplicated subwords}}{\text{non-duplicated subwords}}
in context.
Fractions are categorised into bins, with average surprisal differences computed for each bin. “Support” shows the number of samples per bin.

On the output side, we measure the effect of duplication by first taking the difference in surprisal (negative log probability) assigned to each original subword token
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{t}
by either model
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
(through
\widehat{p}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t})
) or
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
(through
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}_{<t})
).
We then average these delta surprisals within either the set of actually duplicated (treatment) subwords, or the ones not duplicated (control).
We observe that subword duplication leads to an increase in surprisal which is around 50% higher when predicting duplicated tokens compared to non-duplicated ones (see
table

4
).
This might seem unexpected, considering that, e.g., a simple n-gram model’s predictions would not be affected by duplicated outputs.
7
7

7

An
n
-gram’s probabilities are defined by count statistics, and:

\frac{\mathrm{count}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}\|\text{context})}{\mathrm{count}(\text{context})}=\frac{\mathrm{count}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}\|\text{context})}{\mathrm{count}(\text{context})}+\frac{\mathrm{count}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}^{\prime}\|\text{context})}{\mathrm{count}(\text{context})}

Yet, in our LM, the output embeddings of duplicated subwords receive only half as many gradient updates, which could explain the lower performance.
In line with this, we observe that infrequent subwords are affected the most by this performance loss (see
footnote

6
), likely because their output representations are not well aligned after receiving few updates.

To evaluate the impact of duplication on the LM’s input side, we assess how surprisal on subwords changes depending on the number of duplicated (vs non-duplicated) subwords in its context.
Here, we observe no clear trend between the number of duplicates in our model’s full context window and its performance loss on observed subwords (see
fig.

5
).
However, when limited to a more local context of size 16, the number of duplicate tokens seems to have a clear negative impact on prediction performance.

Takeaway 4
.

Infrequent subwords are predicted worse when duplicated, and duplicated tokens in the LM’s local context tend to hurt its predictions.

5.2
Natural Duplication

After exploring the impact of perfect duplicates in LMs, we now turn our attention to the influence of naturally occurring near duplicates on LM performance.
Notably, despite their similarity, near duplicates are seldom perfectly interchangeable.
For example,
_individual
differs from
_individuals
, the
_he
in ‘and he writes’ does not convey the same meaning as
he
in ‘breathe’ (we analyse this in
appendix

C
),
and a
Now
at the start of a sentence can subtly vary from a
now
used mid-sentence.
When merging near duplicates, we lose information about such small differences. If this lost information is relevant for predicting a token
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{t}={\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{t})
, the task should become harder through deduplication.
We have
\mathrm{MI}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}};{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{\mathrm{T}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}})>0
and hence

\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}})>\mathrm{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}})

(17)

which means that even a perfect model would perform worse on the deduplicated data distribution.
This subsection examines whether merging naturally occurring near duplicates yields comparable advantages to merging perfect duplicates.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}
.

5.2.1
Empirical Implementation

We consider four types of near duplicates, defined by their corresponding mapping
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{space}}

ignores leading or trailing whitespace, mapping, e.g.,
_the
to
the
. Around 10% of subwords are merged.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{lower}}

ignores capitalisation, mapping, e.g.,
Now
to
now
. Around 12% of subwords are merged.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{plural}}

ignores plural markings,
8
8

8

For simplicity, we implement it as an easy rule-based mapping: If a subword has a trailing
s
and at least four characters, not counting whitespace, we ignore the
s
. While this approach yields false positives, manual inspections suggests the results are generally acceptable.
mapping, e.g.,
_individuals
to
_individual
. Around 8% of subwords are merged.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{all}}

combines all of the previous mappings, mapping, e.g.,
_Books
to
book
. Around 29% of subwords are merged.

We train models
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
for each of these deduplication mappings
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}
and compare them to the performance of the regular model
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
when evaluated under the same projection.

The first three mappings all reduce the vocabulary size by around 10%.
If the near duplicates were perfectly equivalent, we could expect
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
to perform marginally better than
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
, which is what we observe in the synthetic setting with a low duplication rate (see
fig.

2
).
For the combined
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{all}}
, with 29% near duplicates, we would expect a performance boost corresponding to roughly 5% better data efficiency; or, alternatively, a
\mathrm{PPL}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}
decrease of roughly 0.2 from
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
to
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
(see the results for 40% duplication rate
9
9

9

A ratio of 29% of duplicates in vocabulary
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\Sigma}
corresponds to duplicating around 40% of the initial
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\overline{\Sigma}}
(
\frac{0.4}{1+0.4}\approx{0.29}
).
in
fig.

2
).

Modern large models show even higher duplication rates than our small vocabulary.
Their vocabularies contain around 40% near duplicates under
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{all}}
(see
table

1
).
This translates to duplicating roughly 70% of a canonical vocabulary; in the synthetic case, this corresponds to a potential
data efficiency increase of around 10% due to deduplication, assuming the trends in
fig.

2
apply.
In the following, we examine whether this is plausible by verifying to what extent these trends transfer from perfect duplicates to near duplicates for our models.

Note that, different to the previous subsection where we duplicated our standard vocabulary, here we deduplicate it; this means the
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
in the previous subsection and
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
in this subsection both refer to a “baseline" over our standard vocabulary.

5.2.2
Raw Performance & Duplicate Alignment

We observe that deduplication hurts performance (see
table

5
).
Across all considered
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}
, training
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
on deduplicated data yields worse results than the setting
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
in which near duplicates remain untouched.
The performance degradation is equivalent to training on 5-10% less data.
This is in stark contrast to the trends we observed on perfectly equivalent synthetic duplicates, where deduplication boosts performance.
Near duplicates thus seem to be less equivalent than one might expect, noticeably differing in their semantics.

The near duplicates’ learned embeddings, which tend to contain information about the subwords’ semantics, reflect this discrepancy.
Near duplicates’ embeddings are not nearly as similar as the embeddings of synthetic perfect duplicates, which had a cosine similarity of 0.8: all three types of natural duplicates exhibit average cosine similarities of around 0.4.
This indicates that the model perceives semantic differences between the near duplicates.
As described earlier, if we merge them, we will lose information.
The associated decline in performance indicates that the information is significant.

Takeaway 5
.

Near duplicates are not equivalent and merging them hurts performance.

Mean
\mathbf{\Delta}
Surprisal

Setting

Deduplicated

Not Deduplicated

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{space}}

0.010

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{lower}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{plural}}

Table 6:

Deduplication of half of the vocabulary. Mean difference to baseline surprisal, for subwords in treatment (duplicated) and control (not duplicated) group.

5.2.3
Comparing Deduplicating or Not a Pair

Are predictions worse when more deduplicated tokens are in the context, since more information is “lost”?
We investigate this again through a controlled experiment.
We run experiments where we deduplicate only half of the near duplicates, obtaining a deduplicated treatment group and an unchanged control group.
This setup is similar to the one in the previous subsection, but treatment and control group do not cover the entire vocabulary; both consist only of subwords that have a near duplicate.
To isolate the causal effect of duplication, we investigate differences between these groups.

We first compare the effects of the number of deduplicated subwords and non-deduplicated subwords in the LMs context, i.e., measuring the effect of deduplication on the input side.
An increased number of deduplicated subwords seems to generally lead to a steeper increase in surprisal than an increased number of non-deduplicated subwords, at least in the local context (see
fig.

8
in
appendix

F
).
This suggests that deduplicated subwords in the context do hurt performance, presumably due to the lost information.

Interestingly, deduplicated subwords are also predicted slightly worse on the output side (see
table

6
).
In this setting, the model is forced to learn only one output embedding for each pair of merged near duplicates.
If these merged subwords have different meanings, finding a single embedding to represent both of them might be challenging, as this embedding would need to produce large dot products with the hidden states of contexts from both subwords.

Takeaway 6
.

The presence of merged near duplicates in the local context tends to reduce an LM’s prediction accuracy.

5.2.4
Re-adding Information

If the missing information in the input causes worse predictions, can we improve performance by re-injecting it? If we provide the model with an extra input that allows it to distinguish between merged near duplicates while still sharing their embeddings, could we obtain the benefits of deduplication without the downside of losing information?

To investigate this, we introduce an extra learnable embedding
e_{\text{non-canonical}}
.
This is a single shared vector which is added to the model’s input at every token that corresponds to a non-canonical subword.
(A non-canonical subword
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}
is any subword where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w})\neq{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}
, e.g.,
Now
when operating under
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{lower}}
, since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{lower}}(\text{{Now}})=\text{{now}}\neq\text{{Now}}
.)
As
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{all}}
combines the three different types of deduplication, we use three different learnable embeddings for the
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{all}}
setting, one for each type.

When comparing
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
with and without
e_{\text{non-canonical}}
, such an embedding’s availability significantly improves performance (
\mathrm{p}\leq 0.05
in one-sided t-test) in all four deduplication settings (see
table

5
).
Further,
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
with
e_{\text{non-canonical}}
roughly matches
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
performance for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{space}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{lower}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{plural}}
(differences not statistically signficant with
\mathrm{p}=0.22,0.48,0.72
, respectively, in two-sided t-test).
However, we still do not see the same performance improvements due to deduplication as we observed in the synthetic setting; this is especially clear for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{all}}
which has a higher deduplication rate, but clearly fails to improve performance (
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
with
e_{\text{non-canonical}}
is significantly worse than
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
with
\mathrm{p}\leq 0.05
in two-sided t-test).
Presumably, even if they belong to the same type (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{space}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{lower}}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{plural}}
), near duplicate pairs’ semantic differences are diverse and can thus not be fully captured by a single shared embedding vector.

Takeaway 7
.

Performance losses can be mitigated by accounting for semantic differences of near duplicates via a shared learned embedding.
Still, this approach does not achieve the same benefits as observed when merging perfectly equivalent duplicates.

6
Related Work

Our experiments are partly inspired by a number of works on cross-lingual LM generalisation.
These works also use duplicated vocabularies, terming the duplicates “fake-english”.
Unlike our work, however, they sample entire sequences of either English or “fake-english” tokens
(
K et al. 2020
;
Dufter and Schütze 2020
;
Schäfer et al. 2024
, we perform token-wise i.i.d. sampling;)
. Relatedly,
Huang et al. 2023
’s (
Huang et al. 2023
) lex-invariant LMs can be interpreted as an extreme case of token duplication; essentially creating infinite “fake languages”, they show that LMs can learn, to a certain extent, even in the absence of a fixed set of embeddings.
More similar to our duplication method is the work of
Kharitonov et al. 2021
, who use duplicates sampled at the token level to isolate the effect of vocabulary size when investigating the role of BPE tokenisation in a model’s ability to memorise training data.

Another set of related work studies subword regularisation techniques, such as, e.g., BPE dropout
Kudo 2018
;
Provilkov et al. 2020
.
Certain character sequences can be represented by multiple equivalent sequences of subwords.
These techniques then non-deterministically choose between these equivalent choices at training time, which can lead to improved LMs.
Our perfect duplication experiments, where we train LMs with either of two duplicated tokens, can be seen as similar to these methods.
Unlike them, however, our approach duplicates a model’s vocabulary to introduce this ambiguity, which may explain the fact that our results diverge.
Relatedly, our projected entropy measure (which sums over subword duplicates) is analogous to the marginalisation proposed by
Cao and Rimell 2021
, which sums over spurious tokenisations.

7
Conclusion

In this paper, we investigate to what extent (near) duplicate subwords impact language modelling performance, conducting controlled experiments on synthetic perfect duplicates and natural near duplicates.
We find that LMs can generalise across duplicated subwords, although this incurs extra cost. When operating on a fully duplicated vocabulary, the LM is about 17% less data efficient.
This number depends roughly linearly on the fraction of the vocabulary that is duplicated.
Assuming that roughly 40% of subwords are near duplicates in common LM vocabularies, our findings imply that LMs with improved generalisation across duplicates, e.g., by modelling language at the character-level, could achieve data efficiency gains of up to 10%.
This bound is reached for perfectly equivalent duplicates.
However, we find that natural near duplicates are
not
perfectly equivalent: in practice, the potential for such performance improvements is likely limited.

Limitations

We conduct most of our experiments on models with about 100M parameters, training on roughly 1.2B tokens in English.
Although the trends we identify are consistent in up to 3x larger models and datasets, it is uncertain whether they extend to the scale of modern large language models.
10
10

10

Relatedly,
Bandel et al. 2022
find that larger models trained over extended periods tend to exhibit decreased dependency on lexical overlap.

Furthermore, we have not validated that our results transfer to languages beyond English.

When we study the causal effects of (de)duplication on the input and output side, we do not fully isolate the two effects from each other.
After a manual inspection, it appears that this does not confound the results; e.g., duplicated subwords are not more frequent in the input/context of duplicated subwords than non-duplicated subwords are.
However, to fully exclude the possibility that such patterns affect results, one should run experiments where only the inputs or only the outputs are (de)duplicated.

Finally, while studying natural near duplicates, we only investigate deduplication mappings that can be described by simple heuristics.
Alternatively, one could use more involved methods to also deduplicate, e.g., typos or wordform variations beyond plural forms.

Appendix A
Lemma

1
and Proof

Definition 1

(Time-dependent Conditional Entropy and Mutual Information)
.

We define a time-dependent conditional entropy as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{\leq\mathrm{T}})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathcal{W}}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{C}}}p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})\sum_{t=1}^{|{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}|}\log\frac{1}{p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}_{\leq t})}

(18)

with
\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}})
defined analogously.
Note that, while
p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
stands for the probability of the single event
\{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}\}
, we write
p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t})
to represent the probability of the event composed by the union of all sequences with this prefix, i.e.,
\cup_{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathcal{W}}}\{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}^{\prime}\}
, where
\circ
stands for concatenation.

Accordingly, we define a time-dependent mutual information as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{MI}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}};{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{\mathrm{T}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}})=\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}})-\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{\leq\mathrm{T}}).

(19)

Lemma 1
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}:{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\Sigma}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\overline{\Sigma}}

.
We then have that:

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}})-\mathrm{MI}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}};{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{\mathrm{T}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}})

(20)

Bayes rule

(21d)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathcal{W}}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{C}}}p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})\sum_{t=1}^{|{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}|}\left(\log\frac{1}{p({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}_{<t})}+\log\frac{p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}_{<t})}{p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}_{\leq t})}\right)\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

Split log

(21e)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{C}}}p({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})\sum_{t=1}^{|{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}|}\log\frac{1}{p({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}_{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}_{<t})}+\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathcal{W}}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{C}}}p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})\sum_{t=1}^{|{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}|}\log\frac{p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}_{<t})}{p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}_{\leq t})}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

Split sum

(21f)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}})+\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathcal{W}}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{C}}}p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})\sum_{t=1}^{|{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}|}\log\frac{p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}_{<t})}{p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}_{\leq t})}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

Definition of
\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}})

(21g)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}})+\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{\leq\mathrm{T}})-\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}})\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

Definitions of conditional entropies

(21h)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}})-\mathrm{MI}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}};{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{\mathrm{T}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathrm{C}}}}_{<\mathrm{T}})

Definition of mutual information

(21i)

Note that
p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})>0\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}\implies|{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}|=|{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}}|
, which is required for arriving at
eq.

21f
.
∎

Appendix C
Near Duplicates under
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{space}}

Near duplicates under
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{lower}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{plural}}
are generally very close in meaning.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{lower}}
pairs often consist of the lowercase and the capitalized version of a subword, where the latter might appear at the beginning of sentences. And (sub)word pairs under
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{plural}}
tend to refer to the same lemma. For
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{space}}
, however, the contexts in which subword variants with and without a leading space appear might differ much more: in particular, we observe this with shorter subwords that can appear both as their own word (e.g.,
_he
in ‘and he writes‘) and as a substring of a longer unrelated word (e.g.,
he
in ‘breathe‘).

To quantify how frequently
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathbb{S}}_{\text{space}}
near duplicate pairs show such differences, we manually inspect 100 randomly sampled pairs and note whether the subwords convey comparable meaning in their first occurrences in the training data. We find that 53% of the pairs carry highly similar meaning in both occurrences. Examples include use in compound words (e.g.,
_writing
in isolation vs
writing
in ‘handwriting’, or
_held
in isolation vs
held
in ‘long-held’) or after special characters like newlines, parentheses, or quotation marks (e.g.
_wide
in isolation vs
wide
in ‘(wide […])’). Of the remaining 47%, around half appear in contexts that are clearly different (e.g.,
he
and
_he
as in example above) while the rest are very short subwords where the conveyed meaning is hard to determine (e.g.
_sche
in ‘schemed’ vs
sche
in ‘Nietzsche’).

Appendix D
Effect of Added Parameters for Duplicates

A model
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
over duplicated subwords has more embedding parameters than its deduplicated counterpart
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
.
In the perfect duplication setting, however, these extra parameters should not yield an unfair advantage.
In particular, assume an equiprobable duplicates setting, where

We can show that, in this setting, if there exists an optimal model
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
(which achieves
\widehat{\mathrm{H}}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}})=\mathrm{H}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\mathrm{W}}}})
), then there also exists an equivalent model
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}c}}})
.

without affecting performance.

To see this, note that for the optimal
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
, we have
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})=p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
almost everywhere. Consequently, by Bayes,
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t})=p({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}}_{<t})
.
We thus obtain

\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{458752}{458752}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\hbox to8.35pt{\vbox to8.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.1751pt\lower-4.1751pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}

. The identical input embeddings preserve the value of the hidden state

for any context, which, along with the identical output embeddings, ensures identical model outputs.

Appendix F
Effect of Deduplicated Subwords in the Context

An increased number of deduplicated subwords in the context seems to generally lead to a steeper increase in surprisal than an increased number of non-deduplicated subwords, at least in the local context of 16 tokens (see Figure
fig.

8
). For the full context, the trend is less clear.

Figure 8:

Deduplication of half of the vocabulary. Difference to baseline (
\widehat{p}({\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\boldsymbol{w}})
) surprisal, depending on fraction of (non-)deduplicated subwords in context. Fractions binned and surprisal differences averaged per bin.
To reduce noise and ensure readability, we only plot bins that contain at least 1k predicted tokens.
